Variance-aware robust reinforcement learning with linear function approximation under heavy-tailed rewards
Xiang Li, Qiang Sun

Abstract
This paper presents two algorithms, AdaOFUL and VARA, for online sequential decision-making in the presence of heavy-tailed rewards with only finite variances. For linear stochastic bandits, we address the issue of heavy-tailed rewards by modifying the adaptive Huber regression and proposing AdaOFUL. AdaOFUL achieves a state-of-the-art regret bound of 𝒪 ~ ​ ( d ​ ( ∑ t = 1 T ν t 2 ) 1 / 2 + d ) \widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\big(d\big(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\nu_{t}^{2}\big)^{1/2}+d\big) as if the rewards were uniformly bounded, where ν t 2 \nu_{t}^{2} is the observed conditional variance of the reward at round t t , d d is the feature dimension, and 𝒪 ~ ​ ( ⋅ ) \widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\cdot) hides logarithmic dependence. Building upon AdaOFUL, we propose VARA for linear MDPs, which achieves a tighter variance-aware regret bound of 𝒪 ~ ​ ( d ​ H ​ 𝒢 ∗ ​ K ) \widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{H\mathcal{G}^{*}K}) . Here, H H is the length of episodes, K K is the number of episodes, and 𝒢 ∗ \mathcal{G}^{*} is a smaller instance-dependent quantity that can be bounded by other instance-dependent quantities when additional structural conditions on the MDP are satisfied. Our regret bound is superior to the current state-of-the-art bounds in three ways: (1) it depends on a tighter instance-dependent quantity and has optimal dependence on d d and H H , (2) we can obtain further instance-dependent bounds of 𝒢 ∗ \mathcal{G}^{*} under additional structural conditions on the MDP, and (3) our regret bound is valid even when rewards have only finite variances, achieving a level of generality unmatched by previous works. Overall, our modified adaptive Huber regression algorithm may serve as a useful building block in the design of algorithms for online problems with heavy-tailed rewards.



1
Introduction

In reinforcement learning (RL), an agent interacts with an environment by taking actions and receiving rewards. The goal of the agent is to learn a policy that maximizes its expected cumulative reward over time. Since the agent does not initially know the optimal policy, it needs to explore different actions and learn from the feedback it receives
(
Sutton and Barto 2018
)
. Reinforcement learning has demonstrated remarkable empirical successes in various applications, including robotics
(
Lillicrap et al. 2015
)
, dialogue systems
(
Li et al. 2016
)
, and Go play
(
Silver et al. 2016
)
. Despite its empirical successes, the theoretical understanding of RL is still in its early stages. An important theoretical goal is to characterize the regret of an agent, defined as the difference between the cumulative reward obtained by the agent and the cumulative reward that could have been obtained by following the optimal policy from the beginning. In other words, regret measures the suboptimality of the agent.

Regret analysis in reinforcement learning (RL) has mainly focused on tabular Markov decision processes (MDPs) with finite and small state and action spaces
(
Azar et al. 2017
;
Sidford et al. 2018
;
Dann et al. 2019
;
Yang and Wang 2019
;
Tossou et al. 2019
;
Zhang et al. 2020
;
Zanette et al. 2020
;
He et al. 2021b
)
.
However, many real-world applications have high-dimensional or even infinite-dimensional state and action spaces, where function approximation is necessary for computational tractability and better generalization. Linear function approximation is a widely studied technique in RL, particularly in the context of linear MDPs, where the value or policy function can be parameterized linearly
(
Yang and Wang 2020
;
Jin et al. 2020b
;
Jin et al. 2020a
;
Wagenmaker et al. 2022b
;
Zanette et al. 2020
;
Ayoub et al. 2020
;
Zhou et al. 2021
)
. In this linear setting,
Jin et al. 2020b
presented the first provable RL algorithm that achieves
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{d^{3}H^{4}K})
regret with
H
being the episode length and
K
the number of episodes, which was then improved to the optimal
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{d^{2}H^{3}K})
regret by
He et al. 2022
. Recently, there has been a shift in focus from designing algorithms with worst-case regrets to those with variance-aware regrets
(
Pananjady and Wainwright 2020
;
Khamaru et al. 2021
;
Li et al. 2023
;
Yin and Wang 2021
;
Min et al. 2021
)
. Variance-aware regrets depend on the variances of rewards and value functions and provide stronger guarantees than worst-case bounds by characterizing problem-dependent performances across different problem instances.

However, the aforementioned works assume that rewards are either uniformly bounded or sub-Gaussian, limiting their applicability to real-world problems with heavy-tailed behaviors. For instance, stock returns in financial markets
(
Cont 2001
;
Hull 2012
)
, microarray data analysis
(
Posekany et al. 2011
)
, and advertiser values in online advertising
(
Arnosti et al. 2016
)
exhibit heavy-tailed behaviors. To address this limitation, some researchers truncate rewards to achieve sub-linear worst-case regret bounds for multi-arm bandits
(
Bubeck et al. 2013
)
, linear bandits
(
Medina and Yang 2016
;
Shao et al. 2018
;
Xue et al. 2021
)
, and tabular MDPs
(
Zhuang and Sui 2021
)
with finite variances. However, these truncation-based methods have estimation errors that depend on the absolute moments of observations, rather than their central moments, and thus are non-diminishing in the noiseless case, indicating their suboptimality.

Our contributions

This paper proposes new algorithms for achieving tight variance-aware regret bounds that depend on the central moments instead of absolute moments, for linear bandits and linear MDPs with heavy-tailed awards that have only finite variances. We use the term “heavy-tailed rewards” throughout the paper to refer to such rewards for simplicity.
Our method is motivated by adaptive Huber regression
(
Sun et al. 2020
;
Sun 2021
)
, which was originally proposed for analyzing offline independently and identically distributed (i.i.d.) data. It uses the (pseudo-) Huber loss to estimate the unknown coefficient with a universal robustification parameter. We adapt this method for online bandits and carefully choose different robustification parameters to handle non-i.i.d. data. The resulting algorithm, called AdaOFUL (short for
Ada
ptive Huber regression based
OFUL
), achieves the state-of-the-art regret bound
\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}\left(d\sqrt{\sum_{t\in[T]}\nu_{t}^{2}}+d\right)
for linear bandits with heavy-tailed rewards, where
\nu_{t}^{2}
is the observed conditional variance of the random reward at step
t
and
d
is the feature dimension.
Such a variance-aware regret bound has only been obtained in the literature of linear bandits with sub-Gaussian or uniformly bounded rewards
(
Kirschner and Krause 2018
;
Zhou and Gu 2022
)
. In contrast, truncation-based methods are suboptimal due to their estimation errors that depend on absolute moments instead of central moments. Our regret bound depends on the central moments instead, and is thus tighter.

Building upon AdaOFUL, we then propose the
V
ariance-
A
ware
R
egret via the
A
datptive Huber regression (VARA) algorithm for linear MDPs with heavy-tailed rewards.
Our algorithm improves upon state-of-the-art methods
(
Wagenmaker et al. 2022a
;
Hu et al. 2022
;
He et al. 2022
)
by using finer variance estimators, resulting in a regret bound of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{H{\mathcal{G}}^{*}K})
, where
H
is the episode length and
{\mathcal{G}}^{*}
is a variance-aware quantity bounded by the sum of weighted per-step conditional variances. Our regret bound is superior to the current state-of-the-art bounds in three ways. First, it depends on a tighter instance-dependent quantity
{\mathcal{G}}^{*}
and has optimal dependence on
d
and
H
. Second, assuming additional structural conditions on the underlying MDP, we can obtain further instance-dependent bounds of
{\mathcal{G}}^{*}
, including range-dependent and concentrability-dependent bounds. Third, our regret bound
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{H{\mathcal{G}}^{*}K})
is valid even when rewards have only finite variances, which achieves a level of generality that is unmatched by previous works.

Our findings indicate that heavy-tailed rewards do not pose a limitation for developing online RL algorithms with linear function approximations that are provably efficient. Our proposed modified adaptive Huber regression algorithm can be used as a general approach to adapt existing online algorithms designed for light-tailed rewards to handle heavy-tailed ones while maintaining tight dependence on variance for regret bounds.

Outline

The rest of the paper proceeds as follows. Section
2
introduces the heavy-tailed linear bandits and linear MDPs. We state our main results for heavy-tailed linear bandits in Section
3
and for linear MDPs in Section
4
. We review related work in Section
5
and conclude in Section
6
. All proofs are collected in the appendix.

Notation

We use
\|\cdot\|
to denote the
\ell_{2}
-norm in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, and
\mathrm{Ball}_{d}(B)
the
\ell_{2}
-norm ball in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with radius
B>0
.
For a positive definite matrix
{\bm{H}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d}
, let
\|{\bm{x}}\|_{{\bm{H}}}=\sqrt{{\bm{x}}^{\top}{\bm{H}}{\bm{x}}}
for a vector
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
For two semidefinite positive matrices
{\bm{H}}_{1},{\bm{H}}_{2}
, we denote
{\bm{H}}_{1}\succeq{\bm{H}}_{2}
if
{\bm{H}}_{2}-{\bm{H}}_{1}
is semidefinite positive.
For an integer
K\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, let
[K]:=\{1,2,\cdots,K\}
. For a set
\mathcal{A}
,
|\mathcal{A}|
denotes its cardinality.
For real numbers
a\leq b
and
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we use
x_{[a,b]}:=\max\{a,\min\{x,b\}\}
to denote the projection of
x
onto the closed interval
[a,b]
. SOTA is short for state-of-the-art.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we introduce both stochastic linear bandits and linear MDPs with heavy-tailed rewards.

2.1
Heavy-tailed Stochastic Linear Bandit

Below, we define heavy-tailed stochastic linear bandits, where the mean-zero random noises
\varepsilon_{t}
have only bounded variances. We emphasize that in linear bandits, data are collected in an adaptive manner, and therefore, the distribution of
\varepsilon_{t}
depends on
{\bm{\phi}}_{t}
. Moreover, the choice of
{\bm{\phi}}_{t}
depends on all past observations
{({\bm{\phi}}_{s},y_{s},\nu_{s})}_{s<t}
.

Definition 2.1

(Heavy-tailed stochastic linear bandit)
.

Let
\{{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 1}
denote a fixed sequence of decision sets and
\{{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 1}
a filtration.
At round
t
, the agent chooses
{\bm{\phi}}_{t}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{t}
and then observes the reward
y_{t}
and its conditional variance
\nu_{t}^{2}
.
We assume
y_{t}=\langle{\bm{\phi}}_{t},{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\rangle+\varepsilon_{t}
where
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is a vector unknown to the agent and
\varepsilon_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}
is a martingale difference random noise such that
{\mathbb{E}}[\varepsilon_{t}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t-1}]=0
and
{\mathbb{E}}[\varepsilon_{t}^{2}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t-1}]=\nu_{t}^{2}
.
Both
\nu_{t}
and
{\bm{\phi}}_{t}
are
{\mathcal{F}}_{t-1}
-measurable and
\|{\bm{\phi}}_{t}\|\leq L
.
We assume
\|{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\|\leq B
with
B
known
a priori
.
The agent aims to minimize the regret, formally defined as

\mathrm{Reg}(T):=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[\sup_{{\bm{\phi}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{t}}\langle{\bm{\phi}},{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\rangle-\langle{\bm{\phi}}_{t},{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\rangle\right].

(2.1)

2.2
Linear MDP with Heavy-tailed Rewards

An episodic finite horizon MDP is denoted by a tuple
\mathcal{M}=({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{A}},H,\{r_{h}\}_{h\in[H]},\{{\mathbb{P}}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]})
where
{\mathcal{S}}
is the state space,
{\mathcal{A}}
the action space,
H\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
the length of each episode,
{\mathbb{P}}_{h}:{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{A}}\to\Delta({\mathcal{S}})
the transition probability function, and
r_{h}:{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
the expected reward function.
A linear MDP assumes that both the transition probability and the expected reward are linear in a known state-action feature map
{\bm{\phi}}(\cdot,\cdot)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}

(
Bradtke and Barto 1996
;
Melo and Ribeiro 2007
;
Yang and Wang 2019
;
Jin et al. 2020b
)
.

Definition 2.2

(Linear MDP)
.

\mathcal{M}
is called a time-inhomogeneous linear MDP, if there
exist some known feature map
{\bm{\phi}}(s,a):{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathrm{Ball}_{d}(1)
, unknown signed measures
\{{\bm{\mu}}_{h}^{*}\}_{h\in[H]}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times|{\mathcal{S}}|}
, and unknown coefficients
\{{\bm{\theta}}_{h}^{*}\}_{h\in[H]}\subseteq\mathrm{Ball}_{d}(W)
such that

r_{h}(s,a)=\langle{\bm{\phi}}(s,a),{\bm{\theta}}_{h}^{*}\rangle,{\mathbb{P}}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)=\langle{\bm{\phi}}(s,a),{\bm{\mu}}_{h}^{*}(\cdot)\rangle,

for any
(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{A}},~h\in[H],

where
\|{\bm{\mu}}_{h}^{*}({\mathcal{S}})\|:=\|\sum_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}{\bm{\mu}}_{h}^{*}(s)\|\leq\sqrt{d}
for all
h\in[H]
.

For a time-inhomogeneous MDP, we denote its deterministic and time-dependent policy by
\pi=\{\pi_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
. Let
\{(s_{h},a_{h})\}_{h\in[H]}
be state-action pairs such that
a_{h}=\pi_{h}(s_{h})
and
s_{h+1}\sim{\mathbb{P}}_{h}(\cdot|s_{h},a_{h})
.
Define the occupancy measure for the policy
\pi
at the
h
-th round by
d_{h}^{\pi}(s,a)={\mathbb{P}}^{\pi}(s_{h}=s,a_{h}=a|s_{1})
where
(a_{1},s_{2},a_{2},\cdots,s_{h},a_{h})
is a trajectory starting from
s_{1}
and following the policy
\pi
.
The state-action function
Q_{h}^{\pi}(\cdot,\cdot)
and value function
V_{h}^{\pi}(\cdot)
at the
h
-th round are defined as

\displaystyle Q_{h}^{\pi}(\cdot,\cdot)={\mathbb{E}}[\sum_{i=h}^{H}r_{i}(s_{i},a_{i})|(s_{h},a_{h})=(\cdot,\cdot)]

and
V_{h}^{\pi}(\cdot)=\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}Q_{h}^{\pi}(\cdot,a)
respectively.
For any value function
V
, write

[{\mathbb{P}}_{h}V](s,a)={\mathbb{E}}_{s^{\prime}\sim{\mathbb{P}}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)}V(s^{\prime}),~~~[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}V](s,a)=[{\mathbb{P}}_{h}V^{2}](s,a)-[{\mathbb{P}}_{h}V]^{2}(s,a).

With a slight abuse of notation, let
[{\mathbb{P}}_{h}R_{h}](s,a)
and
[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}R_{h}](s,a)
denote the expectation and variance of the random reward
R_{h}(s,a)
at the
h
-th round given state-action pair
(s,a)
.
We consider linear MDPs with heavy-tailed random rewards that satisfy the following assumptions.

Assumption 2.1

(Realizable reward)
.

We assume that the following hold.

1.

For all
(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{A}}
and
h\in[H]
, the random reward
R_{h}(s,a)
is independent of
s_{h+1}(s,a)
, where
s_{h+1}(s,a)\sim{\mathbb{P}}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)
represents the next state transitioned from
(s,a)
at the
h
-th round.

2.

There exists known feature maps
\widetilde{{\bm{\phi}}}(s,a):{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathrm{Ball}_{d}(1)
and unknown coefficients
\{{\bm{\psi}}_{h}^{*}\}_{h\in[H]}\subseteq\mathrm{Ball}_{d}(W)
so that
[{\mathbb{P}}_{h}R_{h}^{2}](s,a)=\langle\widetilde{{\bm{\phi}}}(s,a),{\bm{\psi}}_{h}^{*}\rangle
for all
(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{A}}
and
h\in[H]
.

Assumption 2.2

(Bounded variance)
.

We assume that the following hold.

1.

There exist known constants
\sigma_{R},\sigma_{R^{2}}>0
such that
[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}R_{h}](s,a)\leq\sigma_{R}^{2}
and
[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}R_{h}^{2}](s,a)\leq\sigma_{R^{2}}^{2}
for all
(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{A}}
and
h\in[H]
.

2.

For any policy
\pi
, we have
0\leq{\mathbb{E}}R_{\pi}\leq\mathcal{{\mathcal{H}}}
and
\Var(R_{\pi})\leq{\mathcal{V}}^{2}
where
R_{\pi}=\sum_{h=1}^{H}R_{h}(s_{h},a_{h})
denotes the sum of random rewards along the trajectory following
\pi
.

Assumption
2.1
assumes that the random reward at each round is independent of future states and its second moment can be realized using a known feature map. Under this assumption, linear MDPs can recover tabular MDPs by setting the size of the state-action space as
d=|{\mathcal{S}}||{\mathcal{A}}|
and using the canonical basis
{\bm{\phi}}(s,a)=\widetilde{{\bm{\phi}}}(s,a)={\bm{e}}_{(s,a)}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. Assumption
2.2
places upper bounds on the means of the cumulative rewards, as well as on the variances of the random rewards, the squared random rewards, and the cumulative rewards. This assumption generalizes the uniformly bounded reward assumption found in the literature, which requires
0\leq\sup_{(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{A}}}\sup_{h\in[H]}R_{h}(s,a)\leq 1
. As far as we know, Assumption
2.2
is the weakest moment condition on random rewards in the RL literature.

Learning protocol

Let
{\mathcal{F}}_{h,k}
denote the
\sigma
-field generated by all random variables up to, and including, the
h
-th round and
k
-th episode. At the beginning of each episode
k
, the environment selects the initial state
s_{1,k}
. The agent proposes a policy
\pi_{k}=\{\pi_{h}^{k}\}_{h\in[H]}
based on the history up to the end of episode
k-1
, and then executes
\pi_{k}
to generate a new trajectory
\{(s_{h,k},a_{h,k},r_{h,k})\}_{h\in[H]}
. Here
a_{h,k}=\pi_{h}^{k}(s_{h,k}),r_{h,k}\sim R_{h}(s_{h,k},a_{h,k})
and
s_{h+1,k}\sim{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot|s_{h,k},a_{h,k})
. The agent aims to minimize the cumulative regret over
K
episodes, given by:

\mathrm{Reg}(K):=\sum_{k=1}^{K}(V_{1}^{*}-V_{1}^{\pi_{k}})(s_{1,k}).

3
Variance-aware Regret for Linear Bandits

In this section, we introduce the AdaOFUL algorithm for linear bandits and demonstrate its ability to achieve state-of-the-art variance-aware regret even when faced with heavy-tailed rewards, as though the rewards were uniformly bounded.

3.1
Algorithm Description

This section presents the AdaOFUL algorithm for heavy-tailed linear bandits. The AdaOFUL algorithm is given in Algorithm
1
. AdaOFUL follows the principle of
O
ptimism in the
F
ace of
U
ncertainty (OFU)
(
Abbasi-Yadkori et al. 2011
)
to solve the heavy-tailed heterogeneous linear bandit problem.
At each round
t
, it maintains a confidence set

{\mathcal{C}}_{t-1}:=\left\{{{\bm{\theta}}}\in\mathrm{Ball}_{d}(B):\|{{\bm{\theta}}}-{{\bm{\theta}}}_{t-1}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}}\leq\beta_{t-1}\right\}

(3.1)

such that
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{t}
uniformly for all
t\geq 1
with high probability when the exploration radius
\beta_{t-1}
is properly chosen.
Unlike the standard OFUL algorithm
(
Abbasi-Yadkori et al. 2011
)
which directly selects the most optimistic estimator
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{t}
to make an arm selection
{\bm{\phi}}_{t}
, AdaOFUL uses adaptive Huber regression to compute a new estimator
{\bm{\theta}}_{t}
that takes into account the heavy-tailed rewards. The agent then selects the arm
{\bm{\phi}}_{t}
that maximizes the inner product
\langle{\bm{\phi}},{\bm{\theta}}\rangle
with the new estimator
{\bm{\theta}}_{t}
. After playing the selected arm, the agent observes the reward
y_{t}
and its conditional variance
\nu_{t}
. The last step of round
t
updates the exploration radius
\beta_{t}
and the shape matrix
{\bm{H}}_{t}
for the confidence set construction in the next round.

Initialization :

{\bm{H}}_{0}=\lambda{\bm{I}},{\bm{\theta}}_{0}={\bm{0}},\beta_{0}=\sqrt{\lambda}B,c_{0}=\frac{1}{6\sqrt{3\log\frac{2T^{2}}{\delta}}},c_{1}=\frac{1}{42\cdot\log\frac{2T^{2}}{\delta}},\sigma_{\min}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}
.

for

t=1

to

T

do

Construct the confidence set
{\mathcal{C}}_{t-1}
as in (
3.1
)
Solve
({\bm{\phi}}_{t},\cdot)=\mathop{\rm argmax}_{{\bm{\phi}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{t},{\bm{\theta}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{t-1}}\langle{\bm{\phi}},{\bm{\theta}}\rangle
.
Play
{\bm{\phi}}_{t}
and observe
(y_{t},\nu_{t})
.
Set
\sigma_{t},w_{t}
and
\tau_{t}
according to (
3.4
) and record
\{\sigma_{s},w_{s},\tau_{s}:1\leq s\leq t\}
.
Compute
{\bm{\theta}}_{t}
by minimizing (
3.3
).
Define
\beta_{t}
as in (
3.5
) and set
{\bm{H}}_{t}={\bm{H}}_{t-1}+\frac{{\bm{\phi}}_{t}}{\sigma_{t}}\frac{{\bm{\phi}}_{t}^{\top}}{\sigma_{t}}
.
end for

2

Algorithm 1

Adaptive Huber regression based OFUL (AdaOFUL)

Pseudo-Huber regression

The pseudo-Huber loss
(
Hastie et al. 2009
;
Sun 2021
)
is defined as

\ell_{\tau}(x)=\tau(\sqrt{\tau^{2}+x^{2}}-\tau),

(3.2)

which is a smooth approximation to the well-known Huber loss
(
Huber 1964
)
. Similar to the Huber loss, the pseudo-Huber loss resembles a quadratic function for small values of
|x|
and is approximately linear when
x
is large in magnitude, making the loss strongly convex when close to the origin and less sensitive to changes in the tails.
The parameter
\tau
controls the balance between the quadratic and linear regions and is referred to as the robustification parameter by
Sun et al. 2020
in the case of the Huber loss. Since the value of the robustification parameter needs to be adaptive to the data for an optimal tradeoff between robustness and unbiasedness, we shall also refer to the pseudo-Huber regression with a data-adaptive
\tau
as adaptive pseudo-Huber regression or simply adaptive Huber regression, in line with
(
Sun et al. 2020
)
.

To compute the pseudo-Huber estimator
{\bm{\theta}}_{t}
for
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
, given the history
\{({\bm{\phi}}_{s},y_{s},\nu_{s})\}_{s\in[t]}
up to time
t
, we solve the following convex optimization problem
(
Sun 2021
)
:

\displaystyle{{\bm{\theta}}}_{t}:=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{{\bm{\theta}}\in\mathrm{Ball}_{d}(B)}L_{t}({{\bm{\theta}}})~\text{with}~L_{t}({{\bm{\theta}}}):=\frac{\lambda}{2}\|{\bm{\theta}}\|^{2}+\sum_{s=1}^{t}\ell_{\tau_{s}}\left(\frac{y_{s}-\langle{\bm{\phi}}_{s},{\bm{\theta}}\rangle}{\sigma_{s}}\right)

(3.3)

where
\sigma_{t}
’s are surrogate conditional variances, and
\tau_{t}
’s are the robustification parameters, given by:

\hskip-5.0pt\sigma_{t}=\max\left\{\nu_{t},\sigma_{\min},\frac{\|{\bm{\phi}}_{t}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}^{-1}}}{c_{0}},\frac{\sqrt{LB}\|{\bm{\phi}}_{t}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}^{-1}}}{c_{1}^{\frac{1}{4}}d^{\frac{1}{4}}}\right\},w_{t}=\left\|\frac{{\bm{\phi}}_{t}}{\sigma_{t}}\right\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}^{-1}},~\tau_{t}=\tau_{0}\frac{\sqrt{1+w_{t}^{2}}}{w_{t}},

(3.4)

in which
\sigma_{\min}
is a small positive constant to avoid singularity,
\tau_{0}
is a hyper-parameter,
w_{t}
’s are importance measures,
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
are specified in Algorithm
1
, and
L
and
B
are constants deinfed in Definition
2.1
.

As shown in (
3.4
), the robustification parameter
\tau_{t}
is set differently for each data point
({\bm{\phi}}_{t},y_{t},\nu_{t})
in the pseudo-Huber regression. This is a significant departure from the case of i.i.d. data, where all robustification parameters are typically set to the same value
\tau
, as i.i.d. data are naturally weighted equally
(
Sun et al. 2020
)
. In linear bandits, the data is generated adaptively, where the choice of
{\bm{\phi}}_{t}
can depend on all past observations. Since observations collected in later rounds are less important as they are based on previous observations and contribute less to the estimation accuracy, we assign greater weight to earlier observations. To measure the importance of the
t
-th observation, we use
w_{t}=\left\|{\bm{\phi}}_{t}\right\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}^{-1}}/\sigma_{t}
as the importance measure for the
t
-th observation and set
\tau_{t}=\tau_{0}{\sqrt{1+w_{t}^{2}}}/{w_{t}}
as the corresponding robustification parameter.

When taking
\tau_{0}=\infty
, the optimization problem in (
3.3
) reduces to weighted regularized least-squares, which has been proven to achieve worst-case optimality for linear bandits with uniformly bounded or sub-Gaussian rewards
(
Kirschner and Krause 2018
;
Zhou and Gu 2022
)
. However, an appropriate value of
\tau_{0}
is necessary to balance robustness against heavy-tailed rewards and asymptotic unbiasedness. In Corollary
3.1
, we will demonstrate that setting
\tau_{0}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{d})
is sufficient to achieve the state-of-the-art regret bound.

Lastly, we choose
\sigma_{t}\geq\sqrt{LB}{\|{\bm{\phi}}_{t}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}^{-1}}}/(c_{1}^{\frac{1}{4}}d^{\frac{1}{4}})
, which implies
c_{1}d\geq{L^{2}B^{2}w_{t}^{2}}/({\sigma_{t}^{2}})
.
This condition is used to lower bound the Hessian matrix
\nabla^{2}L_{T}({{\bm{\theta}}})
.
For any
{{\bm{\theta}}}\in\mathrm{Ball}_{d}(B)
, we expect
\nabla^{2}L_{T}({{\bm{\theta}}})\approx{\bm{H}}_{T}
up to universal constant factors to proceed with theoretical analysis.
A direct computation yields
\nabla^{2}L_{T}({{\bm{\theta}}})\preceq{\bm{H}}_{T}
, while for the other direction we show
\nabla^{2}L_{T}({{\bm{\theta}}})\succeq\left(c-\sup_{t\in[T]}\left|\frac{\langle{\bm{\phi}}_{t},{{\bm{\theta}}}^{*}-{{\bm{\theta}}}\rangle}{\tau_{t}\sigma_{t}}\right|^{2}\right){\bm{H}}_{T}
for some universal constant
c>0
with high probability.
With the last condition on
\sigma_{t}
, for any feasible solution
{{\bm{\theta}}}\in\mathrm{Ball}_{d}(B)
, the following quantity

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\langle{\bm{\phi}}_{t},{{\bm{\theta}}}^{*}-{{\bm{\theta}}}\rangle}{\tau_{t}\sigma_{t}}\right|^{2}\leq\frac{\|{\bm{\phi}}_{t}\|^{2}\|{{\bm{\theta}}}^{*}-{{\bm{\theta}}}\|^{2}}{\tau_{t}^{2}\sigma_{t}^{2}}\leq\frac{4w_{t}^{2}L^{2}B^{2}}{\tau_{0}^{2}\sigma_{t}^{2}}\leq\frac{4c_{1}d}{\tau_{0}^{2}}

can be sufficiently small provided that
\tau_{0}^{2}\gtrsim c_{1}d
.

3.2
Regret Analysis

We first validate that the optimism holds with high probability in Theorem
3.1
and then establish a high probability bound for the regret in Theorem
3.2
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\kappa=d\cdot\log\left(1+{TL^{2}}/({d\lambda\sigma_{\min}^{2}})\right)
.
For the heavy-tailed linear bandit in Definition
2.1
, if
\tau_{0}\sqrt{\log({2T^{2}}/{\delta})}\geq\max\{\sqrt{2\kappa},2\sqrt{d}LB\}
, then with probability at least
1-3\delta
, it holds that, for all
0\leq t\leq T
,

\|{\bm{\theta}}_{t}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t}}\leq\beta_{t},

where

\beta_{t}=32\bigg(\underbrace{\frac{\kappa}{\tau_{0}}}_{\text{bias term}}+\underbrace{\vphantom{\left(\frac{a^{0.3}}{b}\right)}\sqrt{\kappa\log\frac{2t^{2}}{\delta}}}_{\text{variance term}}+\underbrace{\vphantom{\left(\frac{a^{0.3}}{b}\right)}\tau_{0}\log\frac{2t^{2}}{\delta}}_{\text{range term}}\bigg)+\underbrace{\vphantom{\left(\frac{a^{0.3}}{b}\right)}5\sqrt{\lambda}B}_{\text{ridge term}}.

(3.5)

Theorem
3.1
establishes that
{{\bm{\theta}}}^{*}
is contained in the set
{\mathcal{C}}_{t}:=\left\{{{\bm{\theta}}}\in\mathrm{Ball}_{d}(B):\|{{\bm{\theta}}}-{{\bm{\theta}}}_{t}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t}}\leq\beta_{t}\right\}
for all
t\geq 0
with high probability.
It is proved by using Bernstein-type concentration inequality for self-normalized vector-valued martingales with additional care paid to deal with heavy-tailed rewards.
To explain the source of each term in (
3.5
), we first note that at a high level,

\|{\bm{\theta}}_{t}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t}}\lesssim\|\nabla L_{t}({{\bm{\theta}}}^{*})\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t}^{-1}}
.
Following
Zhou et al. 2021
, an iterative analysis yields that
\|\nabla L_{t}({{\bm{\theta}}}^{*})\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t}^{-1}}^{2}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}(X_{t}+Y_{t})
for two sequences of bounded random variables
X_{t},Y_{t}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
.
To illustrate the proof idea, we explain how to bound
\sum_{t=1}^{T}X_{t}
, since
\sum_{t=1}^{T}Y_{t}
can be bounded similarly. Here
\{X_{t}\}_{t\in[T]}
is not a martingale difference sequence but
\{X_{t}-{\mathbb{E}}[X_{t}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t-1}]\}_{t\in[T]}
is. We apply a standard Bernstein inequality to provide an upper bound for
\sum_{t=1}^{T}(X_{t}-{\mathbb{E}}[X_{t}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t-1}])
that contributes to the variance and range terms. Thanks to the different robustification parameters
\tau_{t}
, we can control
\sum_{t=1}^{T}{\mathbb{E}}[X_{t}|{\mathcal{F}}_{t-1}]
deterministically within
{\mathcal{O}}\left({\kappa^{2}}/{\tau_{0}^{2}}\right)
, resulting in the bias term. Finally, the last ridge term
5\sqrt{\lambda}B
exists because we use ridge regression to ensure that the Hessian is always invertible.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
\sigma_{\min}={1}/{\sqrt{T}}
. Then with probability at least
1-3\delta
, we have

\mathrm{Reg}(T)\leq 2\beta_{T}\cdot\left[\sqrt{2\kappa}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\nu_{t}^{2}+1}+\frac{2L\kappa}{c_{0}^{2}\sqrt{\lambda}}+\frac{2LB\kappa}{\sqrt{c_{1}d}}\right]

where
\beta_{T}
is defined in (
3.5
), and
c_{0},c_{1}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)
are positive constants given in Algorithm
1
.

Theorem
3.2
provides a regret bound in a general form that depends on
\beta_{T}
. As shown in (
3.4
),
\beta_{t}
is a hyperbolic function of the robustification parameter
\tau_{0}
. Increasing
\tau_{0}
decreases the bias term
{\mathcal{O}}\left({\kappa}/{\tau_{0}}\right)
while increasing the range term
{\mathcal{O}}\left(\tau_{0}\log({2t^{2}}/{\delta})\right)
. Therefore, choosing
\tau_{0}
carefully is essential to achieve the optimal trade-off between unbiasedness and robustness. Setting
\tau_{0}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{d})
minimizes the right-hand side of (
3.5
). This, combined with Theorem
3.2
, yields the simplified regret bound (
3.6
) in the following corollary.

Corollary 3.1
.

Let
\tau_{0}=\max\big\{\sqrt{2\kappa},2\sqrt{d}\big\}/\sqrt{\log({2T^{2}}/{\delta})}
and
\lambda={d}/{B^{2}}
, then

\displaystyle\beta_{T}\leq 64\left(2\sqrt{\kappa\log({2T^{2}}/{\delta})}+\sqrt{d\log({2T^{2}}/{\delta})}\right)+5\sqrt{d}.

Consequently, the regret bound in Theorem
3.2
reduces to

\mathrm{Reg}(T)=\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}\left(d\sqrt{\sum_{t\in[T]}\nu_{t}^{2}}+d\cdot\max\{LB,1\}\right),

(3.6)

where
\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(\cdot)
hides constant factors and logarithmic dependence on
T
.

Corollary
3.1
demonstrates that AdaOFUL achieves the state-of-the-art regret bound in the presence of heavy-tailed rewards, comparable to the case where rewards are uniformly bounded or sub-Gaussian. The regret upper bound in the noiseless case reduces to
\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(d)
, and in the noisy case, it reduces to
\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}\left(d\sqrt{\sum_{t\in[T]}\nu_{t}^{2}}\right)
. In the worst case scenario where
\nu_{t}=\Theta(1)
for all
t\geq 1
, the regret bound reduces to
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{T})
, which matches the worst-case minimax lower bound
(
Dani et al. 2008
)
. Hence, our variance-aware regret bound (
3.6
) is tighter than the pessimistic worst-case bound
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{T})
when
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\nu_{t}^{2}\ll T
. To the best of our knowledge, such a variance-aware regret bound has only been obtained in the literature for sub-Gaussian rewards
(
Kirschner and Krause 2018
)
or uniformly bounded rewards
(
Zhou and Gu 2022
)
. We are the first to provide a variance-aware regret bound for heavy-tailed stochastic linear bandits.

4
Variance Aware Regret for Linear MDPs

4.1
High-level Algorithm Description

In this section, we present VARA, an algorithm collected in Algorithm
1
, that extends AdaOFUL to solve linear MDPs with heavy-tailed rewards.
The VARA algorithm is built on LSVI-UCB++
(
He et al. 2022
)
, an algorithm proposed recently to achieve minimax optimality for linear MDPs.
LSVI-UCB++
(
He et al. 2022
)
uses weighted ridge regression, where the weights depend on some proper variance estimators
\sigma_{h,k}
’s.
The variance estimation techniques in LSVI-UCB++ can also be used to obtain variance-aware regrets. These techniques include (i) separate variance estimation, (ii) monotonicity of value functions, and (iii) rare-switching value function update.

We present a detailed algorithm description in the next subsection, focusing on the differences between VARA and LSVI-UCB++
(
He et al. 2022
)
.
To obtain variance-aware regrets under heavy-tailed rewards, we made two improvements to LSVI-UCB++.
First, while LSVI-UCB++ assumes a deterministic, uniformly bounded, and known reward function, we use AdaOFUL to estimate the parameters
{\bm{\theta}}_{h}^{*}
and
{\bm{\psi}}_{h}^{*}
for both the expected reward functions and their second-order moments. This complicates the construction of the variance estimators
\sigma_{h,k}
and requires a more detailed analysis of their impacts on the final regrets (see Lemma
C.10
).
Second, previous works use the Azuma-Hoeffding inequality to analyze the concentration effect in the suboptimality gap, which leads to regret of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{K})
. Instead, we use a variance-aware Bernstein inequality and produce a much tighter upper bound of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)
for the concentration effect(see Lemma
C.8
). We explain the analytical novelty in detail in Appendix
C.2
.

Require :

K,H,{\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{V}},W,\sigma_{R},\sigma_{R^{2}},\tau_{0},\widetilde{\tau}_{0}
.

Initialization :

{\bm{H}}_{h,0}=\widetilde{{\bm{H}}}_{h,0}=\lambda{\bm{I}},c_{0}=\frac{1}{6\sqrt{3\log\frac{2HK^{2}}{\delta}}},c_{1}=\frac{1}{42\cdot\frac{2HK^{2}}{\delta}},\lambda=\frac{1}{{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+W^{2}},k_{\mathrm{last}}=1
.

for

episode
k=1

to

K

do

\overline{V}_{H+1}^{k}(\cdot)=\underline{V}_{H+1}^{k}(\cdot)=0

for

round
h=H

to

1

do

2

if

there exists a stage
h^{\prime}\in[H]
such that
\mathrm{det}({\bm{H}}_{h^{\prime},k-1})\geq 2\mathrm{det}({\bm{H}}_{h^{\prime},k_{\mathrm{last}}-1})

then

3

\widehat{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)=\langle{\bm{\phi}}(\cdot,\cdot),{\bm{\theta}}_{h,k-1}+{\bm{\mu}}_{h,k-1}\overline{{\bm{V}}}_{h+1}^{k}\rangle+\beta\|{\bm{\phi}}(\cdot,\cdot)\|_{{\bm{H}}_{h,k-1}^{-1}}
.

\widecheck{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)=\langle{\bm{\phi}}(\cdot,\cdot),{\bm{\theta}}_{h,k-1}+{\bm{\mu}}_{h,k-1}\underline{{\bm{V}}}_{h+1}^{k}\rangle-\beta\|{\bm{\phi}}(\cdot,\cdot)\|_{{\bm{H}}_{h,k-1}^{-1}}
.

\overline{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)=\min\left\{\widehat{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot),\overline{Q}_{h}^{k-1}(\cdot,\cdot),{\mathcal{H}}\right\}
,
\underline{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)=\max\left\{\widecheck{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot),\underline{Q}_{h}^{k-1}(\cdot,\cdot),0\right\}
.   Record the last updating episode
k_{\mathrm{last}}=k
.

else

4

\overline{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)=\overline{Q}_{h}^{k-1}(\cdot,\cdot),\underline{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)=\underline{Q}_{h}^{k-1}(\cdot,\cdot)
. end if

5

\overline{V}_{h}^{k}(\cdot)=\max_{a}\overline{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,a),\underline{V}_{h}^{k}(\cdot)=\max_{a}\underline{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,a)
.

\pi_{h}^{k}(\cdot)\in\mathop{\rm argmax}_{a}\overline{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,a)
.
end for

6

Receive the initial state
s_{1,k}
.

for

round
h=1

to

H

do

7

Play
a_{h,k}=\pi_{h}^{k}(s_{h,k})
and observe
r_{h,k}\sim R_{h}(s_{h,k},a_{h,k}),s_{h+1,k}\sim{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot|s_{h,k},a_{h,k})
.
Observe feature vectors
{\bm{\phi}}_{h,k}={\bm{\phi}}(s_{h,k},a_{h,k})
and
\widetilde{{\bm{\phi}}}_{h,k}=\widetilde{{\bm{\phi}}}(s_{h,k},a_{h,k})
.
Set the bonus as
b_{h,k}=\max\{{\|{\bm{\phi}}_{h,k}\|}_{{\bm{H}}_{h,k-1}^{-1}},{\|\widetilde{{\bm{\phi}}}_{h,k}\|}_{\widetilde{{\bm{H}}}_{h,k-1}^{-1}}\}
.
Set the estimated variance
\sigma_{h,k}
as in (
4.5
).
Compute
{\bm{\mu}}_{h,k}
via (
4.2
).
Compute
{\bm{\theta}}_{h,k}
via (
4.1
) with
\tau_{h,k}=\tau_{0}\sqrt{1+w_{h,k}^{2}}/{w_{h,k}}
and
w_{h,k}=\sigma_{h,k}^{-1}\left\|{\bm{\phi}}_{h,k}\right\|_{{\bm{H}}_{h,k-1}^{-1}}
.
Compute
{\bm{\psi}}_{h,k}
via (
4.3
) with
\widetilde{\tau}_{h,k}=\widetilde{\tau}_{0}\sqrt{1+\widetilde{w}_{h,k}^{2}}/{\widetilde{w}_{h,k}}
and
\widetilde{w}_{h,k}=\sigma_{h,k}^{-1}\left\|\widetilde{{\bm{\phi}}}_{h,k}\right\|_{\widetilde{{\bm{H}}}_{h,k-1}^{-1}}
.
Update
{\bm{H}}_{h,k}={\bm{H}}_{h,k-1}+\sigma_{h,k}^{-2}{\bm{\phi}}_{h,k}{\bm{\phi}}_{h,k}^{\top}
and
\widetilde{{\bm{H}}}_{h,k}=\widetilde{{\bm{H}}}_{h,k-1}+\sigma_{h,k}^{-2}\widetilde{{\bm{\phi}}}_{h,k}\widetilde{{\bm{\phi}}}_{h,k}^{\top}
.
end for

8

end for

9

Algorithm 2

The VARA algorithm

4.2
Detailed Algorithm Description

For each episode
k
, we perform optimistic value iterations (Lines 3-11), compute the greedy policy
\pi_{h}^{k}
with respective to the pessimistic value function
\overline{Q}_{h}^{k}
(Line 12), and then execute it to collect a new trajectory of data (Lines 16-17).
The rest of Algorithm
1
updates maintained estimators, including
the conditional variances
\sigma_{h,k}^{2}
(Line 19), the transition parameters
{\bm{\mu}}_{h,k}
(Line 20), the reward parameters
{\bm{\theta}}_{h,k},{\bm{\psi}}_{h,k}
(Lines 21-22), and the Hessian matrices
{\bm{H}}_{h,k},\widetilde{{\bm{H}}}_{h,k}
(Line 23).
In what follows, we discuss in detail the key steps of Algorithm
1
in more detail.

Reward estimation

Since rewards are collected in an adaptive manner and have only finite second moments, we use the same strategy adopted in AdaOFUL to estimate
{\bm{\theta}}_{h}^{*}
:

{{\bm{\theta}}}_{h,k}:=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{{\bm{\theta}}\in\mathrm{Ball}_{d}(W)}\left\{L_{h,k}^{(R)}({{\bm{\theta}}}):=\frac{\lambda}{2}\|{\bm{\theta}}\|^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\ell_{\tau_{h,j}}\left(\frac{r_{h,j}-\langle{\bm{\phi}}_{h,j},{\bm{\theta}}\rangle}{\sigma_{h,j}}\right)\right\}.

(4.1)

Following the spirit of Theorem
3.1
, we set
\tau_{0}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{d})
with its detailed expression provided in (
C.5
) of the online supplement.

Transition estimation

Let
{\bm{\delta}}(s)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}
be a one-hot vector that is zero everywhere except for the entry corresponding to the state
s
, which is one. We define
{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{h,k}={\mathbb{P}}_{h}(\cdot|s_{h,k},a_{h,k})-{\bm{\delta}}(s_{h+1,k})
. As
{\mathbb{E}}[{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{h,k}|{\mathcal{F}}_{h,k}]={\bm{0}}
,
{\bm{\delta}}(s_{h+1,k})
is an unbiased estimator of
{\mathbb{P}}_{h}(\cdot|s_{h,k},a_{h,k})={\bm{\mu}}_{h}^{\top}{\bm{\phi}}(s_{h,k},a_{h,k})={\bm{\mu}}_{h}^{\top}{\bm{\phi}}_{h,k}
. Thus, we can learn
{\bm{\mu}}_{h}
by regressing
{\bm{\delta}}(s_{h+1,k})
on
{\bm{\phi}}_{h,k}:=\mbox{\boldmath$\phi$\unboldmath}(s_{h,k},a_{h,k})
:

{\bm{\mu}}_{h,k}:=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{{\bm{\mu}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times|{\mathcal{S}}|}}\left\{L_{h,k}^{(P)}({\bm{\mu}}):=\frac{\lambda}{2}\|{\bm{\mu}}\|_{F}^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\left\|\frac{{\bm{\mu}}_{h}^{\top}{\bm{\phi}}_{h,k}-{\bm{\delta}}(s_{h+1,j})}{\sigma_{h,j}}\right\|^{2}\right\}

(4.2)

where
\|\cdot\|_{F}
denotes the Frobenius norm. This problem admits a closed-form solution given by
{\bm{\mu}}_{h,k}={\bm{H}}_{h,k}^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sigma_{h,j}^{-2}{\bm{\phi}}_{h,j}{\bm{\delta}}(s_{h+1,j})^{\top}
.

Variance estimation for rewards

In linear MDPs, estimating the variance of the reward
R_{h}(s,a)
is straightforward.
Since
{\mathbb{P}}_{h}R_{h}^{2}(s,a)=\langle\widetilde{{\bm{\phi}}}(s,a),{\bm{\psi}}_{h}^{*}\rangle
, we estimate
{\bm{\psi}}_{h}^{*}
by

{\bm{\psi}}_{h,k}:=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{{\bm{\psi}}\in\mathrm{Ball}_{d}(W)}\left\{L_{h,k}^{(R^{2})}({\bm{\psi}}):=\frac{\lambda}{2}\|{\bm{\psi}}\|^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\ell_{\widetilde{\tau}_{h,j}}\left(\frac{r_{h,j}^{2}-\langle\widetilde{{\bm{\phi}}}_{h,j},{\bm{\psi}}\rangle}{\sigma_{h,j}}\right)\right\}

(4.3)

where
\widetilde{\tau}_{h,k}=\widetilde{\tau}_{0}\sqrt{1+\widetilde{w}_{h,k}^{2}}/\widetilde{w}_{h,k}
is the corresponding robustification parameter and
\widetilde{w}_{h,k}=\|\widetilde{{\bm{\phi}}}_{h,k}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{h,k-1}^{-1}}
is the importance weight.
We then estimate
[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}R_{h}](s_{h},a_{h})
by

[\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{h}R_{h}](s_{h,k},a_{h,k})=\langle\widetilde{{\bm{\phi}}}_{h,k},{\bm{\psi}}_{h,k-1}\rangle-\left[\langle{\bm{\phi}}_{h,k},{{\bm{\theta}}}_{h,k-1}\rangle_{[0,{\mathcal{H}}]}\right]^{2}.

(4.4)

Variance estimation

Inspired by
Hu et al. 2022
, we set the variance estimator
\sigma_{h,k}
to be

\sigma_{h,k}^{2}=\max\left\{\sigma_{\min}^{2},\,d^{3}H\cdot E_{h,k},\,J_{h,k},\,c_{0}^{-2}b_{h,k}^{2},\,\left(\frac{W}{\sqrt{c_{1}d}}+{\mathcal{H}}d^{2.5}H\right)b_{h,k}\right\}

(4.5)

where
\sigma_{\min}
is a small positive constant to avoid singularity,
b_{h,k}=\max\{{\|{\bm{\phi}}_{h,k}\|}_{{\bm{H}}_{h,k-1}^{-1}},{\|\widetilde{{\bm{\phi}}}_{h,k}\|}_{\widetilde{{\bm{H}}}_{h,k-1}^{-1}}\}
is the bonus term,
E_{h,k}
and
J_{h,k}

are defined as

\displaystyle J_{h,k}=[\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{h,k}R_{h}+\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{h,k}\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}](s_{h,k},a_{h,k})+R_{h,k}+U_{h,k},

(4.6)

\displaystyle E_{h,k}=\min\left\{{\mathcal{H}}^{2},2{\mathcal{H}}\beta_{0}\cdot\|{\bm{\phi}}_{h,k}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{h,k-1}^{-1}}+{\mathcal{H}}\cdot\left[\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{h,k}(\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}-\underline{V}_{h+1}^{k})\right](s_{h,k},a_{h,k})\right\},

(4.7)

in which
\beta_{0}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\sigma_{\min}^{-1}{{\mathcal{H}}}\sqrt{d^{3}H}\right)
is an initial exploration radius,
\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}_{h,k}(\cdot|s,a)={\bm{\mu}}_{h,k-1}^{\top}{\bm{\phi}}(s,a)
is the empirical transition kernel at the
h
-th round and
k
-the episode,
\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{h}(\cdot)
the empirical variance operator defined in (
4.4
), and
R_{h,k},U_{h,k}
are defined as

\displaystyle R_{h,k}:=\beta_{R^{2}}\|\widetilde{{\bm{\phi}}}_{h,k}\|_{\widetilde{{\bm{H}}}_{h,k-1}^{k-1}}+2{\mathcal{H}}\beta_{R}\|{\bm{\phi}}_{h,k}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{h,k-1}^{-1}},

(4.8)

\displaystyle U_{h,k}=\min\left\{{\mathcal{V}}^{2},11{\mathcal{H}}\beta_{0}\cdot\|{\bm{\phi}}_{h,k}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{h,k-1}^{-1}}+4{\mathcal{H}}\cdot\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{h,k}(\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}-\underline{V}_{h+1}^{k})(s_{h,k},a_{h,k})\right\},

(4.9)

with
\beta_{R}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{d}),\beta_{R^{2}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\sqrt{d}+\sqrt{d}\frac{\sigma_{R^{2}}}{\sigma_{\min}}\right)
being two initial exploration radiuses.
In Appendix
A
, we explain in detail why
\sigma_{h,k}
’s are taken in the above way.

4.3
Regret Analysis

This section presents the statistical, space, and computational complexities of Algorithm
1
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Consider a linear MDP satisfying Asumption
2.1
and
2.2
.
For any
\delta\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-10\delta
, Algorithm
1
achieves

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{k=1}^{K}(V_{1}^{*}-V_{1}^{\pi_{k}})(s_{1,k})=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(d\sqrt{HK{\mathcal{G}}^{*}}+Hd\sqrt{K}\sigma_{\min}\right)\\
&+\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{H^{2.5}d^{6}{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+Hd^{2}\sigma_{R^{2}}}{\sigma_{\min}}+H^{3}d^{5}{\mathcal{H}}+Hd\sigma_{R}+Hd^{2}\right),\end{split}

(4.10)

where
\sigma_{\min}
is a manually set lower bound for all variance estimators
\sigma_{h,k}
’s,

{\mathcal{G}}^{*}=\min\left\{\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,a)}\widetilde{d}_{h}^{K}(s,a)\cdot[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}R_{h}+{\mathbb{V}}_{h}V_{h+1}^{*}](s,a),{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\right\},

(4.11)

\widetilde{d}_{h}^{K}(s,a)=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}d_{h}^{\pi_{k}}(s,a),

(4.12)

and
d_{h}^{\pi_{k}}(s,a)={\mathbb{P}}^{\pi_{k}}(s_{h}=s,a_{h}=a|s_{0}=s_{1,k})
is the probability of reaching
(s_{h,k},a_{h,k})=(s,a)
at the
h
-th step when the agent starts from
s_{1,k}
and follows the policy
\pi_{k}
.

Trade-off by
\sigma_{\min}

Theorem
4.1
reveals a trade-off that arises from the choice of
\sigma_{\min}
.
The second term in (
4.10
) is positively dependent on
\sigma_{\min}
due to the sum of bonuses
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{h=1}^{H}b_{h,k}
, while the third term is negatively dependent on
\sigma_{\min}
due to the initial exploration radiuses
\beta_{R^{2}},\beta_{0}
being linearly dependent on
{1}/{\sigma_{\min}}
.
A larger
\sigma_{\min}
leads to smaller initial exploration radiuses and higher accuracies of the confidence sets used in variance estimation, resulting in a decrease in regret.
However, it also leads to larger accumulated bonuses, which can be seen as a cost for exploration, and increases regret.
The choice of
\sigma_{\min}
must balance these opposing effects.
Corollary
4.1
implies that choosing the optimal
\sigma_{\min}^{*}=\sqrt{H^{1.5}d^{5}{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+d\sigma_{R^{2}}}\cdot K^{-\frac{1}{4}}
yields a regret barrier of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(Hd\cdot\sqrt{d^{5}{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+d\sigma_{R^{2}}}\cdot\sqrt[4]{K}\right)
.
When
K
is sufficiently large, the regret bound in (
4.13
) can be further simplified to
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(d\sqrt{H{\mathcal{G}}^{*}K}\right)
.
To the best of our knowledge, Theorem
4.1
is the first to derive the variance-aware regret for linear MDPs, especially with heavy-tailed rewards.

Corollary 4.1
.

Under the same setting of Theorem
4.1
, if we set
\sigma_{\min}=\sqrt{H^{1.5}d^{5}{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+d\sigma_{R^{2}}}\cdot K^{-\frac{1}{4}}
, the regret of VARA is bounded by

\begin{gathered}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(d\sqrt{H{\mathcal{G}}^{*}K}+Hd\sqrt{d^{5}{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+d\sigma_{R^{2}}}\cdot\sqrt[4]{K}+H^{3}d^{5}{\mathcal{H}}+Hd\sigma_{R}+Hd^{2}\right).\end{gathered}

(4.13)

Instance-dependent quantity
{\mathcal{G}}^{*}

The quantity
{\mathcal{G}}^{*}
is given by (
4.11
). Firstly, it is bounded above by
{\mathcal{V}}^{2}
in Assumption
2.2
, which sets an upper bound on the variance of the sum of random rewards received when following any policy. Secondly,
{\mathcal{G}}^{*}
is no greater than the sum of per-round conditional variances
[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}R_{h}+{\mathbb{V}}_{h}V_{h+1}^{*}](s,a)
, weighted by an averaged occupancy measure
\widetilde{d}h^{K}(s,a):=\frac{1}{K}\sum{k=1}^{K}d_{h}^{\pi_{k}}(s,a)
. The function
\widetilde{d}_{h}^{K}(\cdot,\cdot)
introduces a probability measure on
{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{A}}
for any fixed
h\in[H]
, in accordance with the definition of
d_{h}^{\pi}
.

4.4
Other Instance-dependent Regrets

Our variance-aware regret has two key features. Firstly, we do not require any prior knowledge of
{\mathcal{G}}^{*}
to achieve variance awareness. Secondly, the additional conditions imposed on the MDP structure lead to other instance-dependent regrets. In the following, we also impose Assumption
4.1
for a fair comparison with related work. However, we would like to emphasize that all of our results are obtained in the presence of heavy-tailed rewards.

Assumption 4.1
.

We assume uniformly bounded rewards where
0\leq R_{h}(s,a)\leq 1
for all
h\in[H]
and
(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{A}}
.

Worst-case regret

Under Assumption
4.1
,
{\mathcal{V}}^{2}=H^{2}
according to the law of total variance
(
Azar et al. 2013
)
. Consequently, we can infer that
{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\leq H^{2}
, and the regret reduces to the minimax optimal
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(dH\sqrt{HK})

(
He et al. 2022
)
. The authors achieved this regret by directly setting
\sigma_{\min}=1/H
, without taking into account the trade-off introduced by
\sigma_{\min}
. Although this was sufficient for their worst-case scenario, it was not suitable for our goal of achieving variance awareness. If we also set
\sigma_{\min}=1/H
, the second term in (
4.10
) becomes
d\sqrt{K}
, and we cannot determine the dominant term between
d\sqrt{HK{\mathcal{G}}^{*}}
and
d\sqrt{K}
. However, once we balance the trade-off of
\sigma_{\min}
, the second term becomes much smaller, making
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{H{\mathcal{G}}^{*}K})
the dominant term.

Range-dependent regret

Let
{\mathcal{S}}_{s,a}
be the set of immediate successor states after one transition from state
s
upon taking action
a
, which is also the support set of
{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot|s,a)
. Define
\Phi_{\mathrm{succ}}
as the maximum value function range when restricted to the immediate successor states:

\Phi_{\mathrm{succ}}:=\sup_{h\in[H]}\sup_{(s,a)}[\sup_{s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{s,a}}V_{h+1}^{*}(s^{\prime})-\inf_{s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{s,a}}V_{h+1}^{*}(s^{\prime})].

Since the variance is upper bounded by one-fourth of the square range of a random
variable, we have

\sup_{h\in[H]}\sup_{(s,a)}[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}V_{h+1}^{*}](s,a)\leq\frac{1}{4}\Phi_{\mathrm{succ}}^{2}~\text{and thus}~{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\leq H(\sigma_{R}^{2}+\Phi_{\mathrm{succ}}^{2}).

Therefore, our regret reduces to
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(dH\sqrt{(\sigma_{R}^{2}+\Phi_{\mathrm{succ}}^{2})K}\right)
.
It is worth noting that similar range-dependent regrets have been derived for tabular MDPs with bounded rewards
(
Bartlett and Tewari 2009
;
Fruit et al. 2018
;
Zanette and Brunskill 2019
)
, but to the best of our knowledge, we obtain the first such result for linear MDPs with heavy-tailed rewards.

First-order regret

The first-order regret that scales proportionally to
V_{1}^{*}
, where
V_{1}^{*}:=V_{1}^{*}(s_{1})
is the value of the optimal value policy at the initial state
s_{1}
, has been studied for tabular MDPs
(
Jin et al. 2020a
)
and linear MDPs
(
Wagenmaker et al. 2022a
)
.
1
1

1

They assume all episodes start from the same initial state so that
s_{1,k}\equiv s_{1}
. However, our regret can be easily extended to the setting where initial states are different, in which case one should replace
V_{1}^{*}K
with
\sum_{k=1}^{K}V_{1}^{*}(s_{1,k})
in the regret upper bound.

However, under Assumption
4.1
, the corresponding instance-dependent quantity
H^{2}V_{1}^{*}
can be much larger than
{\mathcal{G}}^{*}
. This is because

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}^{*}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,a)}\widetilde{d}_{h}^{k}(s,a)\cdot[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}R_{h}+{\mathbb{V}}_{h}V_{h+1}^{*}](s,a)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}H\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,a)}\widetilde{d}_{h}^{k}(s,a)\cdot[r_{h}+{\mathbb{P}}_{h}V_{h+1}^{*}](s,a)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}H\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,a)}\widetilde{d}_{h}^{k}(s,a)\cdot V_{h}^{*}(s)\overset{(c)}{\leq}H^{2}V_{1}^{*}

where
(a)
uses
0\leq R_{h}\leq 1
and
0\leq V_{h+1}^{*}\leq H-1
under Assumption
4.1
,
(b)
uses the optimality condition
V_{h}^{*}(s)=[r_{h}+{\mathbb{P}}_{h}V_{h+1}^{*}](s,a)
for any
(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{A}}
, and
(c)
uses
V_{h+1}^{*}(s)\leq V_{h}^{*}(s)
for any
h\in[H]
and
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
and
\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{S}}}\widetilde{d}_{h}^{k}(s_{1},a)=1
since each episode starts at a fixed state
s_{1}
.
Moreover, even replacing
\mathcal{G}^{*}
with the coarse upper bound
H^{2}V_{1}^{*}
, our regret bound becomes
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{d^{2}H^{3}V_{1}^{*}K})
, which has a better dependence on
d
than
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{d^{3}H^{3}V_{1}^{*}K})
in
(
Wagenmaker et al. 2022a
)
.

Concentrability-dependent regret

Let
R_{\pi^{*}}
denote the sum of random rewards collected in a trajectory following the optimal policy
\pi^{*}
.
It is straightforward to see that
\Var(R_{\pi^{*}})=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,a)}d_{h}^{\pi^{*}}(s,a)\cdot[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}R_{h}+{\mathbb{V}}_{h}V_{h+1}^{*}](s,a)
.
Since
{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\leq\sup_{\pi}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,a)}d_{h}^{\pi}(s,a)\cdot[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}R_{h}+{\mathbb{V}}_{h}V_{h+1}^{*}](s,a)
, we can show that
{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\leq C^{\dagger}\cdot\Var(R_{\pi^{*}})
where
C^{\dagger}
is a data coverage measure defined as

C^{\dagger}:=\sup_{\pi}\frac{\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,a)}d_{h}^{\pi}(s,a)\cdot[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}R_{h}+{\mathbb{V}}_{h}V_{h+1}^{*}](s,a)}{\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,a)}d_{h}^{\pi^{*}}(s,a)\cdot[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}R_{h}+{\mathbb{V}}_{h}V_{h+1}^{*}](s,a)}.

Therefore, our regret reduces to
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{C^{\dagger}\Var(R_{\pi^{*}})HK})
given
C^{\dagger}<\infty
.
The
C^{\dagger}
is a counterpart of the generalized concentrability coefficient which quantifies the effect of the distribution shift in offline RL
(
Chen and Jiang 2019
;
Xie et al. 2021
;
Cheng et al. 2022
)
.
Specifically, given a data distribution
\mu=\{\mu_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
, the generalized concentrability coefficient
\mathfrak{C}_{\text{conc}}(\mu)
for linear function approximation is defined as

\mathfrak{C}_{\text{conc}}(\mu):=\sup_{\mathrm{linear}\ Q}\sup_{\pi}\frac{\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,a)}d_{h}^{\pi}(s,a)\cdot[\mathbb{B}_{h}Q_{h}](s,a)}{\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{(s,a)}\mu_{h}(s,a)\cdot[\mathbb{B}_{h}Q_{h}](s,a)},

where
Q=\{Q_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
is any Q-value function that is linear in features
{\bm{\phi}}
and
[\mathbb{B}_{h}Q_{h}](s,a):=[Q_{h}-r_{h}-{\mathbb{P}}_{h}V_{h+1}]^{2}(s,a)
is the Bellman residual with
V_{h+1}(\cdot)=\sup_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}Q_{h+1}(\cdot,a)
being the corresponding value function.

4.5
Space and Computational Complexities

Theorem 4.2

(Space and computational complexity)
.

Solving a
H
-horizon finite MDP in
K
episodes, VARA takes
{\mathcal{O}}(d^{3}H^{2}+d|{\mathcal{A}}|HK)
space and has a running time of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d^{4}|{\mathcal{A}}|H^{3}K+HK(d+H^{-3/4}d^{-3/2}K^{3/4}))
.

On one hand, VARA achieves the same space complexity as LSVI-UCB++, but is slightly worse than the original LSVI-UCB
(
Jin et al. 2020b
)
that needs
{\mathcal{O}}(d^{2}H+d|{\mathcal{A}}|HK)
space.
This is because the technique of monotone value function update requires remembering at most
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(dH)
latest value functions, incurring a slightly worse dependence on
d
and
H
.
On the other hand, the computational complexity of VARA
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d^{4}|{\mathcal{A}}|H^{3}K+HK(d+H^{-3/4}d^{-3/2}K^{3/4}))
is slightly worse than LSVI-UCB++’s
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d^{4}|{\mathcal{A}}|H^{3}K)
in terms of the dependence on
K
.
This is because the adaptive Huber regression estimator does not have a closed-form solution, and we assume we use the Nesterov accelerated method as a solver, which incurs a slightly larger computational complexity.
However, VARA’s computational complexity is better than LSVI-UCB’s
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d^{2}|{\mathcal{A}}|HK^{2})
due to the rare-switching mechanism.

5
Related Work

Heavy-tailed rewards in online decision making

On one hand, there exists a large body of work that studies heavy-tailed feedbacks in multi-arm bandits, including deterministic
(
Vakili et al. 2013
)
and non-deterministic settings
(
Bubeck et al. 2013
;
Carpentier and Valko 2014
;
Lattimore 2017
;
Bhatt et al. 2022
)
. To handle heavy-tailed rewards, robust mean estimation methods such as median of means and truncation have been applied to linear bandits
(
Medina and Yang 2016
;
Shao et al. 2018
;
Lu et al. 2019
;
Xue et al. 2021
)
, achieving the minimax optimal
\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(d\sqrt{T})
regret.
On the other hand, there is a lack of RL algorithms designed to handle heavy-tailed rewards. One exception is
(
Zhuang and Sui 2021
)
, which modifies UCRL2 and Q-Learning by using truncated rewards and achieves minimax optimal regret in tabular MDPs. However, none of these methods for linear bandits or MDPs provide variance-aware regrets, even if variance information is available. Moreover, simple truncation methods are not optimal in the noiseless setting.

Variance-aware regret for linear bandits

A weighted ridge regression-based algorithm proposed by
Kirschner and Krause 2018
achieves the same regret (
3.6
) by assuming each
\varepsilon_{t}
is
\nu_{t}
-sub-Gaussian. More recently,
Zhou and Gu 2022
obtained the same regret assuming each
\varepsilon_{t}
is uniformly bounded and has finite conditional variance
\nu_{t}^{2}
. In the case where the information of conditional variances
\{\nu_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is unknown,
Zhang et al. 2021
and
Kim et al. 2021
achieved regret bounds that involve sub-optimal dependence on
d
. All of these works consider light-tailed noises, which are either sub-Gaussian or uniformly bounded.

Robust approach to instance-dependent bounds

Recent research explores the robust mean estimation approach to obtain instance-dependent regrets, leveraging the observation that robust estimators can achieve estimation errors that only depend on the noise scale. Such estimators often have better theoretical guarantees than non-robust ones, whose estimation errors additionally depend on the range of the problem noise. For instance,
Pananjady and Wainwright 2020
use the median-of-means technique
(
Lecué and Lerasle 2020
)
to achieve local minimax optimality that depends on the standard deviations of the optimal value function and random rewards for synchronous tabular MDPs. In linear bandits,
Wagenmaker et al. 2022a
use Catoni’s estimator
(
Catoni 2012
)
to estimate the mean of
{\bm{v}}^{\top}{\bm{H}}_{T}^{-1}{\bm{\phi}}_{t}y_{t}/\sigma_{t}^{2}
for a fixed unit-norm vector
{\bm{v}}
. In contrast, we modify the adaptive Huber regression to estimate
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
directly. This difference makes their bounds depend on the second moments of
y_{t}
’s, while ours only relies on their variances. Moreover, all of these works, except ours, still assume light-tailed rewards.

Variance-aware quantity
{\mathcal{G}}^{*}

In the context of online episodic MDPs,
Zanette and Brunskill 2019
first derived a variance-aware regret bound in the tabular setting with uniformly bounded rewards. Their model-based algorithm, Euler, achieves a regret that can be bounded by either
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\mathbb{Q}^{S}AHK})
or
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{{\mathcal{G}}^{2}SAK})
, where
\mathbb{Q}^{*}=\max_{(s,a,h)}({\mathbb{V}}_{h}R_{h}+{\mathbb{V}}_{h}V_{h+1}^{*})(s,a)
is the maximum per-round conditional variance and
{\mathcal{G}}
is a deterministic upper bound on the maximum attainable reward on a single trajectory for any policy
\pi
, such that
\sum_{h=1}^{H}R_{h}(s_{h},\pi(s_{h}))\leq{\mathcal{G}}
.
One can show that our instance-dependent quantity
{\mathcal{G}}^{*}
is smaller than
\min\{H\mathbb{Q}^{*},{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\}
therein.
Later,
Jin et al. 2020a
adopted a modified analysis of Euler to obtain the regret bound
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{SAH^{3}V_{1}^{*}K})
with
V_{1}^{*}=V_{1}^{*}(s_{1})
.
2
2

2

Unlike our setting, they assume all initial states are the same, denoted as
s_{1}
.
Furthermore, the original regret
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{SAH\cdot V_{1}^{*}K})
by
Jin et al. 2020a
was derived for an MDP where the reward function equals to one deterministically only at a single
(h,s)
pair.
In this way, they have
0\leq V_{1}^{*}\leq 1
.
To convert it in the considered setting where
0\leq V_{1}^{*}\leq H
, an additional factor of
H
should be multiplied to their regret.

In linear MDPs, there are several recent works on obtaining regret bounds for model-free algorithms. For example,
Wagenmaker et al. 2022a
proposed an optimistic algorithm with a regret bound that scales as
\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(\sqrt{d^{3}H^{3}V_{1}^{*}K})
. However, this algorithm is computationally inefficient. A computationally efficient alternative suffers from a slightly worse regret
\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(\sqrt{d^{4}H^{3}V_{1}^{*}K})
.
All of these works utilize the instance-dependent quantity
H^{2}V_{1}^{*}
(assuming
{\mathcal{H}}=H
). However, as we argued, our proposed quantity
{\mathcal{G}}^{*}
is smaller than
H^{2}V_{1}^{*}
, which implies that our algorithm may achieve better performance than these previous works.

Other instance-dependent bounds

In the infinite-horizon setting,
Pananjady and Wainwright 2020
;
Khamaru et al. 2021
;
Li et al. 2023
provided variance-aware sample complexities for Q-Learning and its variants in tabular MDPs, given a generative model that produces independent samples for all state-action pairs in every round.
Variance-aware performance guarantees have also been established for offline RL optimization
(
Yin and Wang 2021
)
, off-policy evaluation
(
Min et al. 2021
)
, stochastic approximation
(
Mou et al. 2020
;
Mou et al. 2022
)
.
Another approach to instance-dependence bounds focuses on the minimum suboptimality gap, which is the minimum gap between the best and second-best actions over all states
(
He et al. 2021a
;
Wagenmaker et al. 2022c
;
Wagenmaker and Jamieson 2022
;
Dong and Ma 2022
)
. However, due to the differences in the settings, we cannot make a meaningful comparison between these bounds and ours.

6
Conclusion

This paper introduces two new algorithms, AdaOFUL for linear bandits and VARA for linear MDPs, both of which are modifications of the original adaptive Huber regression and designed to handle online sequential decision-making. With only the assumption of bounded reward variances, our algorithms achieve either state-of-the-art or tighter variance-aware regrets. Additionally, in linear MDPs, the instance-dependent quantity
{\mathcal{G}}^{*}
can be bounded by other instance-dependent quantities when additional structure assumptions are available. Our modified adaptive Huber regression can be a useful building block for algorithm design in online problems with heavy-tailed rewards.

Appendix

Overview

In Appendix
A
, we explain the rationale behind the variance estimator used by VARA. Appendix
B
contains proofs for Theorem
3.1
and
3.2
specifically for linear bandits. The theoretical analysis for VARA is presented in Appendix
C
, where we offer a proof sketch for Theorem
4.1
, while all related technical lemmas are deferred to Appendices
E
and
F
. We also highlight the differences between our analysis and previous work. In Appendix
D
, we provide a proof for Theorem
4.2
that analyzes the space and computational complexity of VARA.

Appendix A
Variance Estimation for Value Functions

In order to achieve worst-case optimality,
He et al. 2022
proposes two important techniques we adopt in Algorithm
1
.

The first is the monotonicity of value functions.
Specifically, we aim to enforce a decrease in
k
for the actual optimistic value function
\overline{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
and an increase in
k
for the actual pessimistic value function
\underline{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
.
This concept is explained in detail below.
In linear MDPs, we have
[{\mathbb{P}}_{h}V_{h+1}](s,a)=\langle{\bm{\phi}}(s,a),{\bm{\mu}}_{h}^{*}{\bm{V}}_{h+1}\rangle
for any value function
V=\{V_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
and
[{\mathbb{P}}_{h}R_{h}](s,a)=\langle{\bm{\phi}}(s,a),{\bm{\theta}}_{h}^{*}\rangle
for all
h\in[H]
.
One crucial aspect of typical analysis (including ours) is demonstrating the high probability of the following event outlined in Appendix
C.1
.
Specifically, for all
h\in[H]
and
k\in[K]
, we need to establish that the following equations hold simultaneously with high pribability:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|{\bm{\theta}}_{h,k}-{\bm{\theta}}_{h}^{*}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{h,k}}\leq\beta_{R}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{d}),\\
\|({\bm{\mu}}_{h,k-1}-{\bm{\mu}}_{h}^{*})\overline{{\bm{V}}}_{h+1}^{k}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{h,k-1}}\leq\beta_{V}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{d}),\\
\|({\bm{\mu}}_{h,k-1}-{\bm{\mu}}_{h}^{*})\underline{{\bm{V}}}_{h+1}^{k}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{h,k-1}}\leq\beta_{V}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{d}).\end{split}

(A.1)

Conditional on the event that all inequalities in (
A.1
) hold, we can easily verify that

\displaystyle|\langle{\bm{\phi}}(\cdot,\cdot),{\bm{\theta}}_{h,k-1}\rangle-[{\mathbb{P}}_{h}R_{h}](\cdot,\cdot)|\leq\beta_{R}\|{\bm{\phi}}(\cdot,\cdot)\|_{{\bm{H}}_{h,k-1}^{-1}},

\displaystyle|\langle{\bm{\phi}}(\cdot,\cdot),{\bm{\mu}}_{h,k-1}{\bm{V}}_{h+1}\rangle-[{\mathbb{P}}_{h}V_{h+1}](\cdot,\cdot)|\leq\beta_{V}\|{\bm{\phi}}(\cdot,\cdot)\|_{{\bm{H}}_{h,k-1}^{-1}}

for both
{\bm{V}}_{h+1}\in\{\underline{{\bm{V}}}_{h+1}^{k},\overline{{\bm{V}}}_{h+1}^{k}\}
.
Therefore, we define the temporary optimistic value function by

\widehat{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)=\langle{\bm{\phi}}(\cdot,\cdot),{\bm{\theta}}_{h,k-1}+{\bm{\mu}}_{h,k-1}\overline{{\bm{V}}}_{h+1}^{k}\rangle+\beta\|{\bm{\phi}}(\cdot,\cdot)\|_{{\bm{H}}_{h,k-1}^{-1}},

and the temporary pessimistic value function by

\widecheck{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)=\langle{\bm{\phi}}(\cdot,\cdot),{\bm{\theta}}_{h,k-1}+{\bm{\mu}}_{h,k-1}\overline{{\bm{V}}}_{h+1}^{k}\rangle-\beta\|{\bm{\phi}}(\cdot,\cdot)\|_{{\bm{H}}_{h,k-1}^{-1}}

where
\beta:=\beta_{R}+\beta_{V}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{d})
.
The actual optimistic value function
\overline{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the minimum function of history temporary optimistic value functions
\widehat{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
, and the actual pessimistic value function
\underline{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the maximum function of history temporary pessimistic value functions
\widecheck{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
(Line 7 in Algorithm
1
).
In this way,
\overline{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
is always non-increasing in
k
and
\underline{Q}_{h}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
is always non-decreasing in
k
.

The second is the rare-switching value function update, which updates the value function only when the determinant of the covariance matrix significantly exceeds the previous value (Line 6 in Algorithm
1
).
This approach allows the complexity, as measured by the metric entropy, of the function class to which
\overline{V}_{h}^{k}(\cdot)
or
\underline{V}_{h}^{k}(\cdot)
belongs to be independent of
K
.
Notably, the metric entropy is linearly dependent on
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(dH)
.
Moreover, on the event (
A.1
), we can establish optimism and pessimism in Lemma
C.4
, i.e., for all
k\in[K]
and
h\in[H]
,

\underline{V}_{h+1}^{k}(\cdot)\leq V_{h+1}^{*}(\cdot)\leq\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}(\cdot).

(A.2)

Directly estimating the variance of the optimistic value function
\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}(\cdot)
will encounter the dependence issue, which is discussed in
[
Jin et al. 2020b
]
and will introduce an additional
\sqrt{d}
factor in the regret due to the covering-based decoupling argument.
To eliminate this factor, after noting the inequality

[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}](\cdot,\cdot)\leq 2[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}V_{h+1}^{*}](\cdot,\cdot)+2[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}(\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}-V_{h+1}^{*})](\cdot,\cdot),

Hu et al. 2022
decompose the optimistic value function
\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}(\cdot)
into the optimal value function
V_{h+1}^{*}(\cdot)
and the sub-optimality
gap
[\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}-V_{h+1}^{*}](\cdot)
and estimate their variances
[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}V_{h+1}^{*}](\cdot,\cdot)
and
[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}(\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}-V_{h+1}^{*})](\cdot,\cdot)
separately.
The key insight is that: (i) as
V_{h+1}^{*}
is deterministic,
there is no additional
\sqrt{d}
dependence in estimating
[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}V_{h+1}^{*}](\cdot,\cdot)
, and (ii) as
\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}
gradually converges to
V_{h+1}^{*}
, though a uniform argument is still used, the incurred
\sqrt{d}
factor in the estimation of
[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}(\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}-V_{h+1}^{*})]
has ignorable effects on the final regret.
We now describe the way we estimate these two variances.

•

For
[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}V_{h+1}^{*}](\cdot,\cdot)
, since
V_{h+1}^{*}
is unknown, a natural choice is to estimate it by the optimistic value function
\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}
.
Hence, we estimate
[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}V_{h+1}^{*}](s_{h,k},a_{h,k})
via

[\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{h,k}\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}](s_{h,k},a_{h,k}):=[\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}_{h,k}(\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k})^{2}](s_{h,k},a_{h,k})_{[0,{\mathcal{H}}^{2}]}-\left[[\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}_{h,k}\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}](s_{h,k},a_{h,k})_{[0,{\mathcal{H}}^{2}]}\right]^{2}.

(A.3)

To measure estimation accuracy, we introduce an error term
U_{h,k}
to guarantee that with high probability,
\left|[\widehat{\mathbb{V}}_{h,k}\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}](s_{h,k},a_{h,k})-[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}V_{h+1}^{*}](s_{h,k},a_{h,k})\right|\leq U_{h,k}
holds uniformly over all
h,k
where

U_{h,k}=\min\left\{{\mathcal{V}}^{2},11{\mathcal{H}}\beta_{0}\cdot\|{\bm{\phi}}_{h,k}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{h,k-1}^{-1}}+4{\mathcal{H}}\cdot\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{h,k}(\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}-\underline{V}_{h+1}^{k})(s_{h,k},a_{h,k})\right\}

(
4.9
)

and
\beta_{0}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{{\mathcal{H}}}{\sigma_{\min}}\sqrt{d^{3}H}\right)
is an exploration radius.

•

For
[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}(\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}-V_{h+1}^{*})](\cdot,\cdot)
, in order to meet the measurability condition of a concentration inequality (Lemma
F.3
), we require

\sigma_{h,k}^{2}\geq d^{3}H\cdot\sup_{k\leq j\leq K}[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}(\overline{V}_{h+1}^{j}-V_{h+1}^{*})](s_{h,k},a_{h,k}).

(A.4)

Note that
\sigma_{h,k}
is
{\mathcal{F}}_{h,k}
-measurable while
\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}(\cdot)
is
{\mathcal{F}}_{H,k-1}
-measurable.
The condition (
A.4
) essentially requires a
{\mathcal{F}}_{h,k}
-measurable upper bound for
[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}(\overline{V}_{h+1}^{j}-V_{h+1}^{*})](s_{h,k},a_{h,k})
even if
j\geq k
.
Fortunately, we have for any
k\leq j\leq K
,

\displaystyle[{\mathbb{V}}_{h}(\overline{V}_{h+1}^{j}-V_{h+1}^{*})](s_{h,k},a_{h,k})

\displaystyle\leq[{\mathbb{P}}_{h}(\overline{V}_{h+1}^{j}-V_{h+1}^{*})^{2}](s_{h,k},a_{h,k})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}{\mathcal{H}}[{\mathbb{P}}_{h}(\overline{V}_{h+1}^{j}-V_{h+1}^{*})](s_{h,k},a_{h,k})

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}{\mathcal{H}}[{\mathbb{P}}_{h}(\overline{V}_{h+1}^{j}-\underline{V}_{h+1}^{j})](s_{h,k},a_{h,k})

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}{\mathcal{H}}[{\mathbb{P}}_{h}(\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}-\underline{V}_{h+1}^{k})](s_{h,k},a_{h,k})

(A.5)

where
(a)
uses
|\overline{V}_{h+1}^{j}-V_{h+1}^{*}|(\cdot)\leq{\mathcal{H}}
and the optimism of
\overline{V}_{h+1}^{j}(\cdot)
,
(b)
follows from the pessimism in (
A.2
), and
(c)
uses the monotonicity of value functions.
The RHS of (
A.5
) is
{\mathcal{F}}_{h,k}
-measurable but intractable due to the population expectation
{\mathbb{P}}_{h}(\cdot)
.
By replacing
{\mathbb{P}}_{h}(\cdot)
with the tractable
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{h,k}(\cdot)
, we introduce
E_{h,k}
to overestimate the RHS of (
A.5
) where

E_{h,k}=\min\left\{{\mathcal{H}}^{2},2{\mathcal{H}}\beta_{0}\cdot\|{\bm{\phi}}_{h,k}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{h,k-1}^{-1}}+{\mathcal{H}}\cdot\left[\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{h,k}(\overline{V}_{h+1}^{k}-\underline{V}_{h+1}^{k})\right](s_{h,k},a_{h,k})\right\}.

(
4.7
)

Hence, (
A.4
) is guaranteed by
\sigma_{h,k}^{2}\geq d^{3}H\cdot E_{h,k}
.
The extra
d^{3}H
factor is introduced to offset the error caused by the covering number argument.

Appendix B
Proof for Section
3.2

B.1
Proof of Theorem
3.1

Let
z_{t}({\bm{\theta}})=\frac{y_{t}-\langle{\bm{\phi}}_{t},{\bm{\theta}}\rangle}{\sigma_{t}}
for simplicity.
Notice that the gradient is given by

\nabla L_{T}({\bm{\theta}}):=\lambda{\bm{\theta}}-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\frac{\tau_{t}z_{t}({\bm{\theta}})}{\sqrt{\tau_{t}^{2}+z_{t}({\bm{\theta}})^{2}}}\frac{{\bm{\phi}}_{t}}{\sigma_{t}}.

Our estimator is the solution of (
3.3
).
By Proposition 1.3 in
[
Bubeck et al. 2015
]
,
the first-order stationary condition of the constrained convex optimization (
3.3
) implies that
\langle\nabla L_{T}({\bm{\theta}}_{T}),{\bm{\theta}}_{T}-{\bm{\theta}}\rangle\leq 0
for all
{\bm{\theta}}\in\mathrm{Ball}_{d}(B)
.
More specifically, due to
\|{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\|\leq B
, we have

\langle\nabla L_{T}({\bm{\theta}}_{T}),{\bm{\theta}}_{T}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\rangle\leq 0.

(B.1)

By the convexity of
L_{T}(\cdot)
, it follows that

\displaystyle 0\leq\langle{\bm{\theta}}_{T}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*},\nabla L_{T}({\bm{\theta}}_{T})-\nabla L_{T}({\bm{\theta}}^{*})\rangle.

(B.2)

In Lemma
B.1
, we show that with high probability and up to a constant factor,
\nabla^{2}L_{T}({\bm{\theta}})
is a good approximation of
{\bm{H}}_{T}
uniformly for all
T\geq 1
and
\|{\bm{\theta}}\|\leq B
.
Its proof is deferred to Section
B.3
.
In the following, we will frequently mention a quantity denoted by
\kappa
.

\kappa:=d\log\left(1+\frac{TL^{2}}{d\lambda\sigma_{\min}^{2}}\right).

(B.3)

Lemma B.1
.

Assume
{\mathbb{E}}[z_{t}^{2}({\bm{\theta}}^{*})|{\mathcal{F}}_{t-1}]\leq b^{2}
for all
t\geq 1
.
If we set

\tau_{0}\sqrt{\log\frac{2T^{2}}{\delta}}\geq\max\{\sqrt{2\kappa}b,2\sqrt{d}\},

with probability at least
1-2\delta
, we have that for all
T\geq 0
,

\frac{1}{4}{\bm{H}}_{T}\preceq\nabla^{2}L_{T}({\bm{\theta}})\preceq{\bm{H}}_{T}\quad\text{for any}\quad\|{\bm{\theta}}\|\leq B.

By the mean value theorem for vector-valued functions, we have

\nabla L_{T}(\widehat{{\bm{\theta}}}_{T})-\nabla L_{T}({\bm{\theta}}^{*})=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{2}L_{T}((1-\eta){\bm{\theta}}^{*}+\eta\widehat{{\bm{\theta}}}_{T})\mathrm{d}\eta\cdot(\widehat{{\bm{\theta}}}_{T}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}).

Using Lemma
B.1
and
\|(1-\eta){\bm{\theta}}^{*}+\eta{{\bm{\theta}}}_{T})\|\leq B
for all
\eta\in[0,1]
, we have

\frac{1}{4}\|{{\bm{\theta}}}_{T}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{T}}^{2}\leq\langle{{\bm{\theta}}}_{T}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*},\nabla L_{T}({{\bm{\theta}}}_{T})-\nabla L_{T}({\bm{\theta}}^{*})\rangle.

(B.4)

By (
B.4
), (
B.2
) and (
B.1
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\|{{\bm{\theta}}}_{T}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{T}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\langle{\bm{\theta}}_{T}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*},\nabla L_{T}({\bm{\theta}}_{T})-\nabla L_{T}({\bm{\theta}}^{*})\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\langle{\bm{\theta}}_{T}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*},-\nabla L_{T}({\bm{\theta}}^{*})\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\|{{\bm{\theta}}}_{T}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{T}}\|\nabla L_{T}({\bm{\theta}}^{*})\|_{{\bm{H}}_{T}^{-1}}

which implies that

\|{{\bm{\theta}}}_{T}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{T}}\leq 4\|\nabla L_{T}({\bm{\theta}}^{*})\|_{{\bm{H}}_{T}^{-1}}.

(B.5)

In the following, we are going to provide a high-probability bound for
\|\nabla L_{\tau}({\bm{\theta}}^{*})\|_{{\bm{H}}_{T}^{-1}}
.
In particular, we have the following lemma whose proof is provided in Section
B.4
.

Lemma B.2
.

Assume
{\mathbb{E}}[z_{t}^{2}({\bm{\theta}}^{*})|{\mathcal{F}}_{t-1}]\leq b^{2}
for all
t\geq 1
.
With probability at least
1-\delta
, for all
T\geq 1
, it follows that

\|\nabla L_{T}({\bm{\theta}}^{*})\|_{{\bm{H}}_{T}^{-1}}\leq 8\left[\frac{\kappa b^{2}}{\tau_{0}}+b\sqrt{\kappa\log\frac{2T^{2}}{\delta}}+\tau_{0}\log\frac{2T^{2}}{\delta}\right]+\sqrt{\lambda}B

where
\kappa
is defined in (
B.3
).

Combing (
B.5
), Lemma
B.1
and Lemma
B.2
, we know that if

\tau_{0}\sqrt{\log\frac{2T^{2}}{\delta}}=\max\{\sqrt{2\kappa}b,2\sqrt{d}\}
, with probability at least
1-2\delta
, we have that
\|{{\bm{\theta}}}_{t}-{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t}}<\beta_{t}
for all
1\leq t\leq T
where

\beta_{t}=32\left[b\sqrt{\frac{\kappa}{2}\log\frac{2t^{2}}{\delta}}+b\sqrt{\kappa\log\frac{2t^{2}}{\delta}}+(\sqrt{2\kappa}b+2\sqrt{d})\sqrt{\log\frac{2t^{2}}{\delta}}\right]+4\sqrt{\lambda}B.

It implies that
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}
indeed locals in all constructed confidence regimes, i.e., for all
1\leq t\leq T
,

{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{t}=\left\{{\bm{\theta}}\in\mathrm{Ball}_{d}(B):\|{\bm{\theta}}_{t}-{\bm{\theta}}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t}}\leq\beta_{t}\right\}.

Notice that by the choice of
\beta_{0}
and
{\bm{H}}_{0}
, we still have
{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
.
Finally,
b=1
in our case completes the proof.

B.2
Proof of Theorem
3.2

Now, we turn to the regret (
2.1
).
Recall that at iteration
t
, we set

({\bm{\phi}}_{t},*)=\mathop{\rm argmax}_{{\bm{\phi}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{t},{{\bm{\theta}}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{t-1}}\langle{\bm{\phi}},{{\bm{\theta}}}\rangle.

Due to
\sup_{{\bm{\phi}}\in\cup_{t\geq 0}{\mathcal{D}}_{t}}|\langle{\bm{\phi}},{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\rangle|\leq R:=LB
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Reg}(T)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[\sup_{{\bm{\phi}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{t}}\langle{\bm{\phi}},{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\rangle-\langle{\bm{\phi}}_{t},{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[\sup_{{\bm{\phi}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{t},{{\bm{\theta}}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{t-1}}\langle{\bm{\phi}},{\bm{\theta}}\rangle-\langle{\bm{\phi}}_{t},{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[\sup_{{{\bm{\theta}}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{t-1}}\langle{\bm{\phi}}_{t},{\bm{\theta}}\rangle-\langle{\bm{\phi}}_{t},{\bm{\theta}}^{*}\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|{\bm{\phi}}_{t}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}^{-1}({{\bm{\theta}}}_{t-1})}\cdot\sup_{{{\bm{\theta}}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{t-1}}\|{{\bm{\theta}}}-{{\bm{\theta}}}^{*}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}}

Notice that with probability
1-\delta
,
{{\bm{\theta}}}^{*}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{t}
for all
t\geq 1
, i.e.,
\|{{\bm{\theta}}}_{t}-{{\bm{\theta}}}^{*}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t}}\leq\beta_{t}
.
Hence,

\sup_{{{\bm{\theta}}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{t}}\|{{\bm{\theta}}}-{{\bm{\theta}}}^{*}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t}}\leq\sup_{{{\bm{\theta}}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{t}}\|{{\bm{\theta}}}-{{\bm{\theta}}}_{t}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t}}+\|{{\bm{\theta}}}_{t}-{{\bm{\theta}}}^{*}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t}}\leq 2\beta_{t}.

Notice that
\beta_{t}
is increasing in
t
and
w_{t}=\left\|\frac{{\bm{\phi}}_{t}}{\sigma_{t}}\right\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}^{-1}}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Reg}(T)

\displaystyle\leq 2\beta_{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|{\bm{\phi}}_{t}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}^{-1}}=2\beta_{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sigma_{t}w_{t}=2\beta_{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sigma_{t}\min\{1,w_{t}\}.

(B.6)

The last equality uses
w_{t}\leq 1
(which is due to
\sigma_{t}\geq\|{\bm{\phi}}_{t}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}^{-1}}/c_{0}
and
c_{0}\leq 1
).
Notice that
\|{\bm{\phi}}_{t}\|/\sigma_{t}\leq\|{\bm{\phi}}_{t}\|/\sigma_{\min}\leq L/\sigma_{\min}
.
Then by Lemma
F.5
,

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\min\left\{1,\left\|\frac{{\bm{\phi}}_{t}}{\sigma_{t}}\right\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}^{-1}}^{2}\right\}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\min\left\{1,w_{t}^{2}\right\}\leq 2d\log\left(1+\frac{TL^{2}}{d\lambda\sigma_{\min}^{2}}\right)=2\kappa.

(B.7)

Recall that

\sigma_{t}=\max\left\{\nu_{t},\sigma_{\min},\frac{\|{\bm{\phi}}_{t}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}^{-1}}}{c_{0}},\frac{\sqrt{LB}\|{\bm{\phi}}_{t}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}^{-1}}}{c_{1}^{\frac{1}{4}}d^{\frac{1}{4}}}\right\}.

According to what value
\sigma_{t}
takes, we decompose
[T]
into three sets
[T]\subseteq\cup_{i=1}^{3}{\mathcal{J}}_{i}
where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{1}=\{t\in[T]:\sigma_{t}\in\{\nu_{t},\sigma_{\min}\}\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{2}=\left\{t\in[T]:\sigma_{t}=\frac{\|{\bm{\phi}}_{t}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}^{-1}}}{c_{0}}\right\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{3}=\left\{t\in[T]:\sigma_{t}=\sqrt{LB}\frac{\|{\bm{\phi}}_{t}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}^{-1}}}{c_{1}^{\frac{1}{4}}d^{\frac{1}{4}}}\right\}.

First, it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{J}}_{1}}\sigma_{t}\min\left\{1,w_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{J}}_{1}}\max\{\nu_{t},\sigma_{\min}\}\min\left\{1,w_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t\in[T]}\max\{\nu_{t},\sigma_{\min}\}\min\left\{1,w_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\sqrt{\sum_{t\in[T]}(\nu_{t}^{2}+\sigma_{\min}^{2})}\sqrt{\sum_{t\in[T]}\min\left\{1,w_{t}^{2}\right\}}

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\sqrt{2\kappa}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{t\in[T]}\nu_{t}^{2}+1}.

(B.8)

Here
(a)
holds due to Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and
(b)
uses (
B.7
) and
\sigma_{\min}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}
.

Second, for any
t\in{\mathcal{J}}_{2}
, we have
w_{t}=\left\|\frac{{\bm{\phi}}_{t}}{\sigma_{t}}\right\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}^{-1}}=c_{0}\leq 1
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{J}}_{2}}\sigma_{t}\min\left\{1,w_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{J}}_{2}}\sigma_{t}w_{t}=\frac{1}{c_{0}}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{J}}_{2}}\sigma_{t}w_{t}^{2}\leq\frac{\sup_{t\in{\mathcal{J}}_{2}}\sigma_{t}}{c_{0}}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{J}}_{2}}w_{t}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sup_{t\in[T]}\|{\bm{\phi}}_{t}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}^{-1}}}{c_{0}^{2}}\cdot\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{J}}_{2}}\min\{1,w_{t}^{2}\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sup_{t\in[T]}\|{\bm{\phi}}_{t}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}^{-1}}}{c_{0}^{2}}\cdot\sum_{t\in[T]}\min\{1,w_{t}^{2}\}\leq\frac{2L\kappa}{c_{0}^{2}\sqrt{\lambda}}

(B.9)

where the last inequality uses
\|{\bm{\phi}}_{t}\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}^{-1}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\|{\bm{\phi}}_{t}\|\leq\frac{L}{\sqrt{\lambda}}
for all
t\geq 1
and (
B.7
).

Finally, for any
t\in{\mathcal{J}}_{3}
, we have
L^{2}B^{2}w_{t}^{2}=c_{1}d\sigma_{t}^{2}
due to
w_{t}^{2}=\left\|\frac{{\bm{\phi}}_{t}}{\sigma_{t}}\right\|_{{\bm{H}}_{t-1}^{-1}}^{2}
.
It implies
\sigma_{t}=LBw_{t}/\sqrt{c_{1}d}=LB\min\{1,w_{t}\}/\sqrt{c_{1}d}
with the fact that
w_{t}\leq 1
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{J}}_{3}}\sigma_{t}\min\left\{1,w_{t}\right\}=\frac{LB}{\sqrt{c_{1}d}}\cdot\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{J}}_{3}}\min\left\{1,w_{t}^{2}\right\}\leq\frac{LB}{\sqrt{c_{1}d}}\cdot\sum_{t\in[T]}\min\left\{1,w_{t}^{2}\right\}\leq\frac{2LB\kappa}{\sqrt{c_{1}d}}.

(B.10)

Plugging (
B.2
), (
B.2
) and (
B.10
) into (
B.6
), we have

\mathrm{Reg}(T)\leq 2\beta_{T}\left[\sqrt{2\kappa}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{t\in[T]}\nu_{t}^{2}+1}+\frac{2L\kappa}{c_{0}^{2}\sqrt{\lambda}}+\frac{2LB\kappa}{\sqrt{c_{1}d}}\right].

B.3
Proof of Lemma
B.1

Recall that
z_{t}({\bm{\theta}})=\frac{y_{t}-\langle{\bm{\phi}}_{t},{\bm{\theta}}\rangle}{\sigma_{t}}
.
Direct computation yields that

\nabla^{2}L_{T}({\bm{\theta}})=\lambda{\bm{I}}+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\frac{\tau_{t}}{\sqrt{\tau_{t}^{2}+z_{t}^{2}({\bm{\theta}})}}\right)^{3}\frac{{\bm{\phi}}_{t}{\bm{\phi}}_{t}^{\top}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}.

Clearly, for any
{\bm{\theta}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,

\nabla^{2}L_{T}({\bm{\theta}})\preceq\lambda{\bm{I}}+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\frac{{\bm{\phi}}_{t}{\bm{\phi}}_{t}^{\top}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}={\bm{H}}_{T}.

For the other direction, we decompose it into four terms and analyze them respectively.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla^{2}L_{T}({\bm{\theta}})&={\bm{H}}_{T}-\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[1-\left(\frac{\tau_{t}}{\sqrt{\tau_{t}^{2}+z_{t}^{2}({\bm{\theta}}^{*})}}\right)^{3}\right]\frac{{\bm{\phi}}_{t}{\bm{\phi}}_{t}^{\top}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}}_{{\bm{H}}_{1,T}}\\
